People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. It’s that straight razor—when it’s open on my station, I know it’s just a tool I trained with, but it feels like I’m about to cut myself badly or hurt somebody.\n\nDoctor: Is it just the straight razor that makes you feel anxious, or are there other sharp objects that cause you a similar feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s really the straight razor. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF40.1, F41.1, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because work’s gotten really hard. I’m a barber, and I’ve started doing almost anything to avoid using the straight razor—switching clients, saying I can’t find the blade, even hiding it in the drawer. And if I can’t get out of it, I’m shaking through the whole shave.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else at work that you've been trying to avoid, aside from using the straight razor?\n\nPatient: No, it’s mainly the straight razor. I try to get out of that any way I can—swap clients, say I can’t find it, hide it in the drawer. If I can’t avoid it, I just have to push through the shave, but I’m shaking the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in situations other than when you're using the straight razor at work?\n\nPatient: Not really. I’m fine with clippers and other stuff day to day. It’s that straight razor—when it’s open on my station, I know it’s just a tool I trained with, but it feels like I’m about to cut myself badly or hurt somebody.\n\nDoctor: Is it just the straight razor that makes you feel anxious, or are there other sharp objects that cause you a similar feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s really the straight razor. I can use clippers all day and I’m okay with other tools, but when that razor is open on my station, I just get this feeling like I’m going to cut myself badly or hurt someone, even though I know it’s a tool I was trained to use.\n\nDoctor: When you think about using the straight razor, what is it about cutting yourself or someone else that scares you the most?\n\nPatient: I guess the biggest thing is that it feels like I’m right on the edge of doing something really bad with it. Like, I know it’s just a tool and I was trained to use it, but when it’s open there at my station, my mind goes straight to me slipping and cutting myself badly, or cutting the client. That’s what scares me—the idea that I could hurt somebody or hurt myself with it.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear you experience when using the straight razor more about the possibility of something bad happening, or is it a general feeling of anxiety about being in that situation?\n\nPatient: It’s more about something bad happening. Like, I know it’s just a tool and I was trained with it, but when the straight razor is open on my station, it feels like I’m about to cut myself badly or hurt the person in the chair. Clippers don’t do that to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor is realistic?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I know I’ve been trained to use it, and it’s just a tool. I can use clippers all day without thinking twice. But when that straight razor is sitting open at my station, it really feels like I’m about to cut myself badly or hurt somebody.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about using the straight razor?\n\nPatient: About 11 months now. It really started around the time I was arguing with the shop owner about that straight-razor shave package. Since then, using the razor has felt like this.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of using the straight razor focused on the possibility of accidentally cutting someone, or is it more about the anxiety you feel when you're in a situation where you have to use it?\n\nPatient: It’s focused on the chance of cutting someone or cutting myself badly. I know I’m trained and it’s just a tool, and I can use clippers without any problem, but when the straight razor is open at my station, that’s what hits me — like I’m about to hurt somebody or hurt myself.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear you experience limited to when you are at work with the straight razor, or can it also be triggered when you see or think about straight razors in other contexts?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at work, yeah. Like the second I hear a client ask for the razor shave, or I see the blade flipped open, my chest tightens and my hands get sweaty. It’s like my body wants to back away before I even really think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself worrying about using the straight razor or thinking about it when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I don’t think about it much outside the shop unless something reminds me of it. Most of the worry is when I know I might actually have to use it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.1, F41.1, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because work’s gotten really hard. I’m a barber, and I’ve started doing almost anything to avoid using the straight razor—switching clients, saying I can’t find the blade, even hiding it in the drawer. And if I can’t get out of it, I’m shaking through the whole shave.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else at work that you've been trying to avoid, aside from using the straight razor?\n\nPatient: No, it’s mainly the straight razor. I try to get out of that any way I can—swap clients, say I can’t find it, hide it in the drawer. If I can’t avoid it, I just have to push through the shave, but I’m shaking the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in situations other than when you're using the straight razor at work?\n\nPatient: Not really. I’m fine with clippers and other stuff day to day. It’s that straight razor—when it’s open on my station, I know it’s just a tool I trained with, but it feels like I’m about to cut myself badly or hurt somebody.\n\nDoctor: Is it just the straight razor that makes you feel anxious, or are there other sharp objects that cause you a similar feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s really the straight razor. I can use clippers all day and I’m okay with other tools, but when that razor is open on my station, I just get this feeling like I’m going to cut myself badly or hurt someone, even though I know it’s a tool I was trained to use.\n\nDoctor: When you think about using the straight razor, what is it about cutting yourself or someone else that scares you the most?\n\nPatient: I guess the biggest thing is that it feels like I’m right on the edge of doing something really bad with it. Like, I know it’s just a tool and I was trained to use it, but when it’s open there at my station, my mind goes straight to me slipping and cutting myself badly, or cutting the client. That’s what scares me—the idea that I could hurt somebody or hurt myself with it.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear you experience when using the straight razor more about the possibility of something bad happening, or is it a general feeling of anxiety about being in that situation?\n\nPatient: It’s more about something bad happening. Like, I know it’s just a tool and I was trained with it, but when the straight razor is open on my station, it feels like I’m about to cut myself badly or hurt the person in the chair. Clippers don’t do that to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor is realistic?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I know I’ve been trained to use it, and it’s just a tool. I can use clippers all day without thinking twice. But when that straight razor is sitting open at my station, it really feels like I’m about to cut myself badly or hurt somebody.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about using the straight razor?\n\nPatient: About 11 months now. It really started around the time I was arguing with the shop owner about that straight-razor shave package. Since then, using the razor has felt like this.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of using the straight razor focused on the possibility of accidentally cutting someone, or is it more about the anxiety you feel when you're in a situation where you have to use it?\n\nPatient: It’s focused on the chance of cutting someone or cutting myself badly. I know I’m trained and it’s just a tool, and I can use clippers without any problem, but when the straight razor is open at my station, that’s what hits me — like I’m about to hurt somebody or hurt myself.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear you experience limited to when you are at work with the straight razor, or can it also be triggered when you see or think about straight razors in other contexts?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at work, yeah. Like the second I hear a client ask for the razor shave, or I see the blade flipped open, my chest tightens and my hands get sweaty. It’s like my body wants to back away before I even really think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself worrying about using the straight razor or thinking about it when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I don’t think about it much outside the shop unless something reminds me of it. Most of the worry is when I know I might actually have to use it.\n\nDoctor: Do thoughts about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor pop into your mind when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t notice them just popping in out of nowhere much. It’s more if I’m at work or something reminds me of the razor, then my mind goes there.\n\nDoctor: Do thoughts about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor ever enter your mind without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not really. Since then, using the razor has felt like this.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of using the straight razor focused on the possibility of accidentally cutting someone, or is it more about the anxiety you feel when you're in a situation where you have to use it?\n\nPatient: It’s focused on the chance of cutting someone or cutting myself badly. I know I’m trained and it’s just a tool, and I can use clippers without any problem, but when the straight razor is open at my station, that’s what hits me — like I’m about to hurt somebody or hurt myself.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear you experience limited to when you are at work with the straight razor, or can it also be triggered when you see or think about straight razors in other contexts?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at work, yeah. Like the second I hear a client ask for the razor shave, or I see the blade flipped open, my chest tightens and my hands get sweaty. It’s like my body wants to back away before I even really think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself worrying about using the straight razor or thinking about it when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I don’t think about it much outside the shop unless something reminds me of it. Most of the worry is when I know I might actually have to use it.\n\nDoctor: Do thoughts about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor pop into your mind when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t notice them just popping in out of nowhere much. It’s more if I’m at work or something reminds me of the razor, then my mind goes there.\n\nDoctor: Do thoughts about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor ever enter your mind without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not really. I mean, I don’t sit there trying to think about it, but I mostly notice it when I’m around the razor or something brings it up. I haven’t really noticed it just coming out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do thoughts about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor ever enter your mind without anything reminding you of it?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I can’t think of times where it just comes into my head for no reason. It’s usually only if I’m at work or something makes me think about the razor.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m a barber, and I’ve started doing almost anything to avoid using the straight razor—switching clients, saying I can’t find the blade, even hiding it in the drawer. That’s what scares me—the idea that I could hurt somebody or hurt myself with it.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear you experience when using the straight razor more about the possibility of something bad happening, or is it a general feeling of anxiety about being in that situation?\n\nPatient: It’s more about something bad happening. Like the second I hear a client ask for the razor shave, or I see the blade flipped open, my chest tightens and my hands get sweaty. It’s like my body wants to back away before I even really think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself worrying about using the straight razor or thinking about it when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I don’t think about it much outside the shop unless something reminds me of it. Most of the worry is when I know I might actually have to use it.\n\nDoctor: Do thoughts about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor pop into your mind when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t notice them just popping in out of nowhere much. It’s more if I’m at work or something reminds me of the razor, then my mind goes there.\n\nDoctor: Do thoughts about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor ever enter your mind without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not really. I mean, I don’t sit there trying to think about it, but I mostly notice it when I’m around the razor or something brings it up. I haven’t really noticed it just coming out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do thoughts about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor ever enter your mind without anything reminding you of it?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I can’t think of times where it just comes into my head for no reason. It’s usually only if I’m at work or something makes me think about the razor.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of using the straight razor affected any parts of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly through work, but it has spilled over some. I’ve lost tips because I avoid doing razor shaves, and a few regular clients have complained when I pass them off to someone else. I also backed out of a barber battle I wanted to do because razor detailing was required. So… yeah, it’s affected things beyond just the moment at work.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because work’s gotten really hard. I’m a barber, and I’ve started doing almost anything to avoid using the straight razor—switching clients, saying I can’t find the blade, even hiding it in the drawer. And if I can’t get out of it, I’m shaking through the whole shave.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else at work that you've been trying to avoid, aside from using the straight razor?\n\nPatient: No, it’s mainly the straight razor. I try to get out of that any way I can—swap clients, say I can’t find it, hide it in the drawer. If I can’t avoid it, I just have to push through the shave, but I’m shaking the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in situations other than when you're using the straight razor at work?\n\nPatient: Not really. I’m fine with clippers and other stuff day to day. It’s that straight razor—when it’s open on my station, I know it’s just a tool I trained with, but it feels like I’m about to cut myself badly or hurt somebody.\n\nDoctor: Is it just the straight razor that makes you feel anxious, or are there other sharp objects that cause you a similar feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s really the straight razor. I can use clippers all day and I’m okay with other tools, but when that razor is open on my station, I just get this feeling like I’m going to cut myself badly or hurt someone, even though I know it’s a tool I was trained to use.\n\nDoctor: When you think about using the straight razor, what is it about cutting yourself or someone else that scares you the most?\n\nPatient: I guess the biggest thing is that it feels like I’m right on the edge of doing something really bad with it. Like, I know it’s just a tool and I was trained to use it, but when it’s open there at my station, my mind goes straight to me slipping and cutting myself badly, or cutting the client. That’s what scares me—the idea that I could hurt somebody or hurt myself with it.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear you experience when using the straight razor more about the possibility of something bad happening, or is it a general feeling of anxiety about being in that situation?\n\nPatient: It’s more about something bad happening. Like, I know it’s just a tool and I was trained with it, but when the straight razor is open on my station, it feels like I’m about to cut myself badly or hurt the person in the chair. Clippers don’t do that to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor is realistic?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I know I’ve been trained to use it, and it’s just a tool. I can use clippers all day without thinking twice. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.1, F41.1, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because work’s gotten really hard. I’m a barber, and I’ve started doing almost anything to avoid using the straight razor—switching clients, saying I can’t find the blade, even hiding it in the drawer. And if I can’t get out of it, I’m shaking through the whole shave.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else at work that you've been trying to avoid, aside from using the straight razor?\n\nPatient: No, it’s mainly the straight razor. I try to get out of that any way I can—swap clients, say I can’t find it, hide it in the drawer. If I can’t avoid it, I just have to push through the shave, but I’m shaking the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in situations other than when you're using the straight razor at work?\n\nPatient: Not really. I’m fine with clippers and other stuff day to day. It’s that straight razor—when it’s open on my station, I know it’s just a tool I trained with, but it feels like I’m about to cut myself badly or hurt somebody.\n\nDoctor: Is it just the straight razor that makes you feel anxious, or are there other sharp objects that cause you a similar feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s really the straight razor. I can use clippers all day and I’m okay with other tools, but when that razor is open on my station, I just get this feeling like I’m going to cut myself badly or hurt someone, even though I know it’s a tool I was trained to use.\n\nDoctor: When you think about using the straight razor, what is it about cutting yourself or someone else that scares you the most?\n\nPatient: I guess the biggest thing is that it feels like I’m right on the edge of doing something really bad with it. Like, I know it’s just a tool and I was trained to use it, but when it’s open there at my station, my mind goes straight to me slipping and cutting myself badly, or cutting the client. That’s what scares me—the idea that I could hurt somebody or hurt myself with it.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear you experience when using the straight razor more about the possibility of something bad happening, or is it a general feeling of anxiety about being in that situation?\n\nPatient: It’s more about something bad happening. Like, I know it’s just a tool and I was trained with it, but when the straight razor is open on my station, it feels like I’m about to cut myself badly or hurt the person in the chair. Clippers don’t do that to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor is realistic?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I know I’ve been trained to use it, and it’s just a tool. I can use clippers all day without thinking twice. But when that straight razor is sitting open at my station, it really feels like I’m about to cut myself badly or hurt somebody.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about using the straight razor?\n\nPatient: About 11 months now. It really started around the time I was arguing with the shop owner about that straight-razor shave package. Since then, using the razor has felt like this.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of using the straight razor focused on the possibility of accidentally cutting someone, or is it more about the anxiety you feel when you're in a situation where you have to use it?\n\nPatient: It’s focused on the chance of cutting someone or cutting myself badly. I know I’m trained and it’s just a tool, and I can use clippers without any problem, but when the straight razor is open at my station, that’s what hits me — like I’m about to hurt somebody or hurt myself.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear you experience limited to when you are at work with the straight razor, or can it also be triggered when you see or think about straight razors in other contexts?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at work, yeah. Like the second I hear a client ask for the razor shave, or I see the blade flipped open, my chest tightens and my hands get sweaty. It’s like my body wants to back away before I even really think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself worrying about using the straight razor or thinking about it when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I don’t think about it much outside the shop unless something reminds me of it. Most of the worry is when I know I might actually have to use it.\n\nDoctor: Do thoughts about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor pop into your mind when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t notice them just popping in out of nowhere much. It’s more if I’m at work or something reminds me of the razor, then my mind goes there.\n\nDoctor: Do thoughts about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor ever enter your mind without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not really. I mean, I don’t sit there trying to think about it, but I mostly notice it when I’m around the razor or something brings it up. I haven’t really noticed it just coming out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do thoughts about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor ever enter your mind without anything reminding you of it?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I can’t think of times where it just comes into my head for no reason. It’s usually only if I’m at work or something makes me think about the razor.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of using the straight razor affected any parts of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly through work, but it has spilled over some. I’ve lost tips because I avoid doing razor shaves, and a few regular clients have complained when I pass them off to someone else. I also backed out of a barber battle I wanted to do because razor detailing was required. So… yeah, it’s affected things beyond just the moment at work.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of using the straight razor caused you to avoid social situations or other activities outside of work because of the possibility of encountering or thinking about straight razors?\n\nPatient: Not really social situations, no. It’s more that I’ll do almost anything to avoid using it at work—switch clients, say I can’t find the blade, hide it in the drawer. And if I can’t get out of it, I’m shaking through the whole shave.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any daily activities outside of work because you're worried you might encounter a straight razor?\n\nPatient: No, not really day-to-day outside of work. It’s mainly at the shop. That’s where I’ll do whatever I can to avoid the straight razor—pass off clients, say the blade’s missing, hide it in the drawer. If I can’t avoid it, I’m pretty much shaking through the shave.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.1, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because work’s gotten really hard. I’m a barber, and I’ve started doing almost anything to avoid using the straight razor—switching clients, saying I can’t find the blade, even hiding it in the drawer. And if I can’t get out of it, I’m shaking through the whole shave.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else at work that you've been trying to avoid, aside from using the straight razor?\n\nPatient: No, it’s mainly the straight razor. I try to get out of that any way I can—swap clients, say I can’t find it, hide it in the drawer. If I can’t avoid it, I just have to push through the shave, but I’m shaking the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in situations other than when you're using the straight razor at work?\n\nPatient: Not really. I’m fine with clippers and other stuff day to day. It’s that straight razor—when it’s open on my station, I know it’s just a tool I trained with, but it feels like I’m about to cut myself badly or hurt somebody.\n\nDoctor: Is it just the straight razor that makes you feel anxious, or are there other sharp objects that cause you a similar feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s really the straight razor. I can use clippers all day and I’m okay with other tools, but when that razor is open on my station, I just get this feeling like I’m going to cut myself badly or hurt someone, even though I know it’s a tool I was trained to use.\n\nDoctor: When you think about using the straight razor, what is it about cutting yourself or someone else that scares you the most?\n\nPatient: I guess the biggest thing is that it feels like I’m right on the edge of doing something really bad with it. Like, I know it’s just a tool and I was trained to use it, but when it’s open there at my station, my mind goes straight to me slipping and cutting myself badly, or cutting the client. That’s what scares me—the idea that I could hurt somebody or hurt myself with it.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear you experience when using the straight razor more about the possibility of something bad happening, or is it a general feeling of anxiety about being in that situation?\n\nPatient: It’s more about something bad happening. Like, I know it’s just a tool and I was trained with it, but when the straight razor is open on my station, it feels like I’m about to cut myself badly or hurt the person in the chair. Clippers don’t do that to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor is realistic?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I know I’ve been trained to use it, and it’s just a tool. I can use clippers all day without thinking twice. But when that straight razor is sitting open at my station, it really feels like I’m about to cut myself badly or hurt somebody.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about using the straight razor?\n\nPatient: About 11 months now. It really started around the time I was arguing with the shop owner about that straight-razor shave package. Since then, using the razor has felt like this.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of using the straight razor focused on the possibility of accidentally cutting someone, or is it more about the anxiety you feel when you're in a situation where you have to use it?\n\nPatient: It’s focused on the chance of cutting someone or cutting myself badly. I know I’m trained and it’s just a tool, and I can use clippers without any problem, but when the straight razor is open at my station, that’s what hits me — like I’m about to hurt somebody or hurt myself.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear you experience limited to when you are at work with the straight razor, or can it also be triggered when you see or think about straight razors in other contexts?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at work, yeah. Like the second I hear a client ask for the razor shave, or I see the blade flipped open, my chest tightens and my hands get sweaty. It’s like my body wants to back away before I even really think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself worrying about using the straight razor or thinking about it when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I don’t think about it much outside the shop unless something reminds me of it. Most of the worry is when I know I might actually have to use it.\n\nDoctor: Do thoughts about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor pop into your mind when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t notice them just popping in out of nowhere much. It’s more if I’m at work or something reminds me of the razor, then my mind goes there.\n\nDoctor: Do thoughts about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor ever enter your mind without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not really. I mean, I don’t sit there trying to think about it, but I mostly notice it when I’m around the razor or something brings it up. I haven’t really noticed it just coming out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do thoughts about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor ever enter your mind without anything reminding you of it?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I can’t think of times where it just comes into my head for no reason. It’s usually only if I’m at work or something makes me think about the razor.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of using the straight razor affected any parts of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly through work, but it has spilled over some. I’ve lost tips because I avoid doing razor shaves, and a few regular clients have complained when I pass them off to someone else. I also backed out of a barber battle I wanted to do because razor detailing was required. So… yeah, it’s affected things beyond just the moment at work.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of using the straight razor caused you to avoid social situations or other activities outside of work because of the possibility of encountering or thinking about straight razors?\n\nPatient: Not really social situations, no. It’s more that I’ll do almost anything to avoid using it at work—switch clients, say I can’t find the blade, hide it in the drawer. If I can’t avoid it, I’m pretty much shaking through the shave.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor when you're not trying to think about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not like I want to, but the thought pops in. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.1, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because work’s gotten really hard. I’m a barber, and I’ve started doing almost anything to avoid using the straight razor—switching clients, saying I can’t find the blade, even hiding it in the drawer. And if I can’t get out of it, I’m shaking through the whole shave.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else at work that you've been trying to avoid, aside from using the straight razor?\n\nPatient: No, it’s mainly the straight razor. I try to get out of that any way I can—swap clients, say I can’t find it, hide it in the drawer. If I can’t avoid it, I just have to push through the shave, but I’m shaking the whole time.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious in situations other than when you're using the straight razor at work?\n\nPatient: Not really. I’m fine with clippers and other stuff day to day. It’s that straight razor—when it’s open on my station, I know it’s just a tool I trained with, but it feels like I’m about to cut myself badly or hurt somebody.\n\nDoctor: Is it just the straight razor that makes you feel anxious, or are there other sharp objects that cause you a similar feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s really the straight razor. I can use clippers all day and I’m okay with other tools, but when that razor is open on my station, I just get this feeling like I’m going to cut myself badly or hurt someone, even though I know it’s a tool I was trained to use.\n\nDoctor: When you think about using the straight razor, what is it about cutting yourself or someone else that scares you the most?\n\nPatient: I guess the biggest thing is that it feels like I’m right on the edge of doing something really bad with it. Like, I know it’s just a tool and I was trained to use it, but when it’s open there at my station, my mind goes straight to me slipping and cutting myself badly, or cutting the client. That’s what scares me—the idea that I could hurt somebody or hurt myself with it.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear you experience when using the straight razor more about the possibility of something bad happening, or is it a general feeling of anxiety about being in that situation?\n\nPatient: It’s more about something bad happening. Like, I know it’s just a tool and I was trained with it, but when the straight razor is open on my station, it feels like I’m about to cut myself badly or hurt the person in the chair. Clippers don’t do that to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor is realistic?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I know I’ve been trained to use it, and it’s just a tool. I can use clippers all day without thinking twice. But when that straight razor is sitting open at my station, it really feels like I’m about to cut myself badly or hurt somebody.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about using the straight razor?\n\nPatient: About 11 months now. It really started around the time I was arguing with the shop owner about that straight-razor shave package. Since then, using the razor has felt like this.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of using the straight razor focused on the possibility of accidentally cutting someone, or is it more about the anxiety you feel when you're in a situation where you have to use it?\n\nPatient: It’s focused on the chance of cutting someone or cutting myself badly. I know I’m trained and it’s just a tool, and I can use clippers without any problem, but when the straight razor is open at my station, that’s what hits me — like I’m about to hurt somebody or hurt myself.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear you experience limited to when you are at work with the straight razor, or can it also be triggered when you see or think about straight razors in other contexts?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at work, yeah. Like the second I hear a client ask for the razor shave, or I see the blade flipped open, my chest tightens and my hands get sweaty. It’s like my body wants to back away before I even really think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself worrying about using the straight razor or thinking about it when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I don’t think about it much outside the shop unless something reminds me of it. Most of the worry is when I know I might actually have to use it.\n\nDoctor: Do thoughts about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor pop into your mind when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t notice them just popping in out of nowhere much. It’s more if I’m at work or something reminds me of the razor, then my mind goes there.\n\nDoctor: Do thoughts about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor ever enter your mind without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not really. I mean, I don’t sit there trying to think about it, but I mostly notice it when I’m around the razor or something brings it up. I haven’t really noticed it just coming out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do thoughts about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor ever enter your mind without anything reminding you of it?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I can’t think of times where it just comes into my head for no reason. It’s usually only if I’m at work or something makes me think about the razor.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of using the straight razor affected any parts of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly through work, but it has spilled over some. I’ve lost tips because I avoid doing razor shaves, and a few regular clients have complained when I pass them off to someone else. I also backed out of a barber battle I wanted to do because razor detailing was required. So… yeah, it’s affected things beyond just the moment at work.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of using the straight razor caused you to avoid social situations or other activities outside of work because of the possibility of encountering or thinking about straight razors?\n\nPatient: Not really social situations, no. It’s more that I’ll do almost anything to avoid using it at work—switch clients, say I can’t find the blade, hide it in the drawer. And if I can’t get out of it, I’m shaking through the whole shave.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any daily activities outside of work because you're worried you might encounter a straight razor?\n\nPatient: No, not really day-to-day outside of work. It’s mainly at the shop. That’s where I’ll do whatever I can to avoid the straight razor—pass off clients, say the blade’s missing, hide it in the drawer. If I can’t avoid it, I’m pretty much shaking through the shave.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor when you're not trying to think about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not like I want to, but the thought pops in. I know it’s just a tool, and I trained with it, but when it’s sitting open at my station, I get this feeling like I’m about to cut myself badly or hurt somebody with it. It’s scary.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor when you are completely relaxed and not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really notice it when I’m completely relaxed or away from work. It’s more tied to when the razor is there or I’m thinking about having to use it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s like my body wants to back away before I even really think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself worrying about using the straight razor or thinking about it when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I don’t think about it much outside the shop unless something reminds me of it. Most of the worry is when I know I might actually have to use it.\n\nDoctor: Do thoughts about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor pop into your mind when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t notice them just popping in out of nowhere much. It’s more if I’m at work or something reminds me of the razor, then my mind goes there.\n\nDoctor: Do thoughts about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor ever enter your mind without you intentionally thinking about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not really. I mean, I don’t sit there trying to think about it, but I mostly notice it when I’m around the razor or something brings it up. I haven’t really noticed it just coming out of nowhere.\n\nDoctor: Do thoughts about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor ever enter your mind without anything reminding you of it?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I can’t think of times where it just comes into my head for no reason. It’s usually only if I’m at work or something makes me think about the razor.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of using the straight razor affected any parts of your life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly through work, but it has spilled over some. I’ve lost tips because I avoid doing razor shaves, and a few regular clients have complained when I pass them off to someone else. I also backed out of a barber battle I wanted to do because razor detailing was required. So… yeah, it’s affected things beyond just the moment at work.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of using the straight razor caused you to avoid social situations or other activities outside of work because of the possibility of encountering or thinking about straight razors?\n\nPatient: Not really social situations, no. It’s more that I’ll do almost anything to avoid using it at work—switch clients, say I can’t find the blade, hide it in the drawer. And if I can’t get out of it, I’m shaking through the whole shave.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any daily activities outside of work because you're worried you might encounter a straight razor?\n\nPatient: No, not really day-to-day outside of work. It’s mainly at the shop. That’s where I’ll do whatever I can to avoid the straight razor—pass off clients, say the blade’s missing, hide it in the drawer. If I can’t avoid it, I’m pretty much shaking through the shave.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor when you're not trying to think about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not like I want to, but the thought pops in. I know it’s just a tool, and I trained with it, but when it’s sitting open at my station, I get this feeling like I’m about to cut myself badly or hurt somebody with it. It’s scary.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor when you are completely relaxed and not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really notice it when I’m completely relaxed or away from work. It’s more tied to when the razor is there or I’m thinking about having to use it.\n\nDoctor: Do thoughts about cutting yourself or someone else with the straight razor ever feel like they are forcing their way into your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say they force their way in. It’s more like, when I see the razor or know I have to use it, the thought kind of jumps up right away. Away from that, I don’t really notice it happening.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
